Y=newoy-G\n    D=np.zeros_like(E)\n    for x,y,d in zip(X,Y,dt[rr,cc]):\n        ax=min(rx,d*fx); ay=min(ry,d)\n        if ax<2 or ay<2: continue\n        cv2.ellipse(D,(int(round(x*4)),int(round(y*4))),(int(ax*4),int(ay*4)),0,0,360,1,-1,lineType=cv2.LINE_8,shift=2)\n    near=cv2.dilate(D,cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(2*band+1,2*band+1)))\n    F=D|(E&near)\n    return smooth(F,3),ox,newoy\nif __name__=='__main__':\n    import sys\n    out={}\n    for l in sys.argv[1]:\n        Wstd=L[l]['box'][2]*S;     "text": "I rebuilt n, a, o, s and w with the even-stroke method. specimen-even-stroke-test.png sets them in three columns: the current 1966-based letters, the even-stroke test, and the Palo Alto sign forms. The test came out much closer to the current letters than I expected.\n\nI measured stroke thickness all the way around each letter. The figure is the thinnest point divided by the thickest, so 1.0 would be perfectly even:\n\n| | Current | Even-stroke | Sign forms |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| n | 0.77 | 0.81 | 0.77 |\n| a | 0.73 | 0.79 | 0.62 |\n| o | 0.93 | 0.89 | 0.77 |\n| s | 0.77 | 0.78 | 0.70 |\n\nThe even-stroke method makes the n's shoulder and the a's bowl a little fuller, but the gain is small. By this measure the sign letters are actually the least even of the three.\n\n**Why it made little difference.** The Series D lowercase is only 2\u20138% narrower than the 1966 standard, so my squeeze barely distorted it. What you're noticing in the sign letters isn't more even strokes but a different drawing. Their curves are fuller and rounder: the a's bowl, the n's shoulder, the s's rounded ends and the w's rounded inner points. The 1966 shapes are flatter-sided and more geometric, so narrowed even slightly they read as squeezed, even though the strokes measure about the same.\n\n**Where even strokes would really help** is the Series C font. Its 18 lowercase letters not on the signs were squeezed to about 78% width. That's enough to visibly thin the curves and diagonals, and they sit next to the eight sign letters.\n\nFor the Series D font, if the sign letters' character is what you like, using the sign shapes for all 26 letters would get closer than any stroke correction. That would mean reshaping the 18 letters not on the signs to match the sign letters' round shoulders and full bowls.\n\nWould you rather I apply the even-stroke rebuild to the Series C font's 18 squeezed letters, or work on giving the Series D lowercase the sign letters' rounder shapes throughout?",

    "stop_timestamp": "2026-10-09T01:36:52.980858Z",
    "flags": null,
    "type": "text",
    "text": "I've built the full family: five fonts, Series B through F, each with the 1966 capitals and figures for that series and a matching even-stroke lowercase. They're HighwayEven1966-B.ttf through HighwayEven1966-F.ttf, and specimen-family.png sets the same lines in all five.\n\n**How the lowercase is made.** One engine draws all five widths from the 1966 lower case sheets:\n1. It traces each letter's centre line and removes the stray branches that tracing creates in corners.\n2. It narrows or widens that centre line.\n3. It redraws the stroke at a constant thickness along it, with horizontal strokes about 10% thinner than vertical ones, as on the signs.\n4. Terminal cuts, flat tops and baselines, and corners come from the 1966 outline, so they stay sharp.\n\nEach new width takes only a few seconds, so any other width in between or beyond is easy to add.\n\n**How the five widths line up.** I measured all 36 capitals and figures in each series against Series E. In our earlier work the lowercase narrowed less than the capitals did: the sign lowercase is about 0.78 of standard width, while Series C capitals are about 0.69 of Series E. I kept that same relationship for every series.\n\n| Series | Capital width vs E | Capital stem | Lowercase width vs 1966 standard | Lowercase stem |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| B | 0.54 | 91 | 0.65 | 85 |\n| C | 0.69 | 102 | 0.78 | 95 |\n| D | 0.85 | 110 | 0.89 | 102 |\n| E | 1.00 | 122 | 1.00 | 113 |\n| F | 1.15 | 132 | 1.10 | 123 |\n\nEvery lowercase stem is about 93% of its capitals' stem, the relationship you approved earlier.\n\n**Limitations**\n- The capitals and figures are straight tracings of the 1966 sheets, not redrawn by the engine. They're already close to an even stroke, so they sit well with the lowercase.\n- The lowercase uses the 1966 letter shapes throughout. The Palo Alto sign shapes for a, e, h, o, r, s, w and y aren't in this family yet. The engine can take them as starting shapes, so that's a natural next step if you want it.\n- Spacing scales with width and weight. The sheets don't specify it, so it's my judgment, and the widest and narrowest series would benefit from your eye.\n\n**A bug this exposed.** Round letters like O, 0 and C were being given too much space on their left. Wider letters showed it badly enough to see. It's fixed in all five new fonts. The same bug affects the earlier HighwayNarrow1966C.ttf, HighwayNarrow1966D.ttf and HighwayNarrow1966D-SignForms.ttf, so I can rebuild those too if you're still using them.",
    "citations": []
  }
]

In Series E, for example, the h's ascender was 126 units against the 113 stem.\n\nThe cause was the slanted cut on top of the 1966 ascenders. The straightening now stops just below where the hook starts, so the stem below the crossbar is still straight and the hook keeps its curve and terminal.\n\nAt very large sizes I can see two small things on the f.     "text": "The Series E lowercase is now traced directly from the 1966 lower case sheets rather than generated by the even-stroke engine. In seriesE-lowercase-vs-1966.png, each new letter's outline is drawn in red over the scanned 1966 letter in gray.\n\nThe outlines follow the drawings closely. For most letters, the area where outline and scan disagree is only 2\u20136% of the letter. That's about the level of edge roughness in the scans themselves, which only have about 115 pixels of x-height. The z differs most, at about 12%, mostly along its diagonal. The letters keep their 1966 proportions exactly: x-height, ascender and descender depths, slanted ascender cuts, spurs and round dots.\n\n**One consequence: the lowercase is now heavier than the capitals.** The 1966 lowercase stem is about 137 units, against about 122 for the Series E capitals. You can see this in specimen-E.png. As far as I know, that lowercase was drawn to go with Series E(M), a heavier-stroked version of Series E, not the standard Series E capitals. I've kept the drawings' weight as they are, since matching them exactly is the point. If your 1966 manual has the Series E(M) capital sheets, using those would give the pairing the drawings were made for.\n\nI also fixed a small notch on the left edge of the Series E capital F. It came from a small circular mark printed on top of the letter in the 1966 drawing.\n\nThe B, C, D and F fonts still use the even-stroke engine, so they don't match the 1966 drawings this closely. The engine-built Series E lowercase is saved, so I can go back to it if you change your mind.",
    "citations": []
  }

Straight edges stay straight, every corner stays as sharp as in the reference font, and nothing gets retraced. I'd expect that to fix the x and make the light font match the reference corner for corner. It's a bigger change to how I build the light font, so I'd do it as a separate step if you'd like.",

    "type": "text",
    "text": "The extra flares are gone in HighwayEven1966-E.ttf, while the stem weight and sharp corners stay as they were last time.\n\n**Cause.** Some corners in the reference outlines are made of several very short segments rather than one clean point. These include the slanted ascender tops of d, h and k, the k's arm, the top corners and feet of x, and the top of the y. When every edge is moved inward by 9 units, those tiny segments can point in slightly different directions. Moving them inward pushed a little sliver outward instead, which you saw as a flare.\n\n**Fix.** I now check each thinned letter against the result of shaving 9 units evenly off every edge of the reference letter. Anything that sticks out more than 5 units past that is trimmed away. That's enough to remove the flares, but not enough to round the sharp inside corners, such as where the t's crossbar meets its stem.\n\nThe stems still measure about 123 units, matching the capitals. The x's top corners are still sharp, and the small curve at the d's top left corner and the slight flare at the x's bottom left foot are both in the 1966 drawings.",

Y=newoy-G(oy-Rr)\\n        for x,y,d0 in zip(X,Y,dd):\\n            ax=min(rx,d0*max(f,1)); ay=min(ry,d0)\\n            if ax<3 or ay<3: continue\\n            cv2.ellipse(D,(int(round(x*4)),int(round(y*4))),(int(ax*4),int(ay*4)),0,0,360,1,-1,shift=2)\\n    D=smooth(D,3)\\n    Ed=cv2.dilate(E,cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(2*band+1,2*band+1)))\\n    F=E|(D&Ed)\\n    F=smooth(F,1.5)\\n    return F,pad,newoy\\nSER={'B':(0.654,91),'C':(0.78,102),'D':(0.89,110),'F':(1.10,132)}\\nif __name__=='__main__':\\n    import square\\n    s=sys.argv[1];     "text": "I've derived the B, C, D and F lowercase from the new high-resolution Series E lowercase, so all four now come from the outlines you've been checking. specimen-family.png shows all five widths, and the E font is unchanged.\n\n**Approach.** Each width is built from the full-weight 1966 lowercase in two layers:\n- **Narrowed or widened outline:** the letter scaled horizontally, then corrected so its stems and horizontal strokes come out at the target weight. This keeps the 1966 terminals, cuts and corners.\n- **Even-stroke layer:** a constant-width stroke drawn along each letter's centre line. Where curves or diagonals would otherwise come out thin, it fills them out, but it can only add up to 4 units per side, so it can't round off ends.\n\nAfterwards I square the same corners as in Series E: the stem ends of a, b, d, u, g, m, n, p, q and r, the f and t crossbars, and the top of the x. Lowercase stems now match each series' capitals:\n\n| Series | Width vs E lowercase | Capital stem | Lowercase stem |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| B | 0.65 | 91 | 91\u201392 |\n| C | 0.78 | 102 | 101\u2013103 |\n| D | 0.89 | 110 | 108\u2013111 |\n| E | 1.00 | 122 | 123 |\n| F | 1.10 | 132 | 129\u2013133 |\n\n**Limitations**\n- **Sharpness.** At large sizes the derived widths aren't as crisp as E. Curves like o and c are slightly uneven, and the tops of v, w, x and y and the t's crossbar are a little soft. That's because narrowing is still done on a picture of each letter, not on the outlines. Narrowing the outlines directly, as I did for the E thinning, would fix it, though that's a bigger job.\n- **Capitals.** The B, C, D and F capitals are still traced from the original low-resolution page scans, so they're softer than the Series E capitals. If you can scan those pages at the same high resolution, I can rebuild them the way I did Series E.\n- **Spacing** still uses each series' earlier settings, so P, S and the other adjustments carry over.",
